package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here

public class CalculatorTest {
    @Test
    public void test_add_two_positive_integers() {
        // If the method add is called with two positive integers (e.g., 2 and 3), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (5). This verifies the basic functionality described as 'Returns the sum of a and b'.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(2, 3);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_two_negative_integers() {
        // If the method add is called with two negative integers (e.g., -2 and -3), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (-5). This ensures the method correctly handles negative inputs as part of the general 'sum' requirement.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(-2, -3);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_mixed_sign_integers_positive_result() {
        // If the method add is called with a positive integer and a negative integer where the positive magnitude is greater (e.g., 5 and -2), then the method should return the positive arithmetic sum (3). This tests the correct handling of mixed signs.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(5, -2);
        assertEquals(3, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_mixed_sign_integers_negative_result() {
        // If the method add is called with a positive integer and a negative integer where the negative magnitude is greater (e.g., 2 and -5), then the method should return the negative arithmetic sum (-3). This tests the correct handling of mixed signs.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(2, -5);
        assertEquals(-3, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_additive_inverse() {
        // If the method add is called with an integer and its negation (e.g., 5 and -5), then the method should return 0. This verifies the mathematical property of additive inverses within the sum operation.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(5, -5);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_as_first_operand() {
        // If the method add is called with 0 as the first operand and any integer as the second (e.g., 0 and 5), then the method should return the second operand (5). This verifies the additive identity property.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(0, 5);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_as_second_operand() {
        // If the method add is called with any integer as the first operand and 0 as the second (e.g., 5 and 0), then the method should return the first operand (5). This verifies the additive identity property.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(5, 0);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_to_zero() {
        // If the method add is called with 0 for both operands, then the method should return 0. This is a specific edge case of the additive identity.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(0, 0);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_integer_max_value_with_zero() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MAX_VALUE and 0, then the method should return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This verifies behavior at the upper boundary of the int type.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 0);
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_integer_min_value_with_zero() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MIN_VALUE and 0, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This verifies behavior at the lower boundary of the int type.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 0);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_integer_max_value_with_one_overflows() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MAX_VALUE and 1, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE. Since the documentation does not specify overflow handling, this tests the default Java integer overflow wrap-around behavior.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_add_integer_min_value_with_negative_one_overflows() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MIN_VALUE and -1, then the method should return Integer.MAX_VALUE. Since the documentation does not specify overflow handling, this tests the default Java integer overflow wrap-around behavior.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, -1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, result);
    }


}
package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here
public class CalculatorTest {

    @Test
    public void test_add_two_positive_integers() {
        // If the method add is called with two positive integers (e.g., 2 and 3), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (5). This verifies the basic functionality described as 'Returns the sum of a and b'.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(2, 3);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_two_negative_integers() {
        // If the method add is called with two negative integers (e.g., -2 and -3), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (-5). This ensures the method correctly handles negative inputs as part of the general 'sum' requirement.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(-2, -3);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_mixed_sign_integers_positive_result() {
        // If the method add is called with a positive integer and a negative integer where the positive magnitude is greater (e.g., 5 and -2), then the method should return the positive arithmetic sum (3). This tests the correct handling of mixed signs.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(5, -2);
        assertEquals(3, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_mixed_sign_integers_negative_result() {
        // If the method add is called with a positive integer and a negative integer where the negative magnitude is greater (e.g., 2 and -5), then the method should return the negative arithmetic sum (-3). This tests the correct handling of mixed signs.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(2, -5);
        assertEquals(-3, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_additive_inverse() {
        // If the method add is called with an integer and its negation (e.g., 5 and -5), then the method should return 0. This verifies the mathematical property of additive inverses within the sum operation.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(5, -5);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_as_first_operand() {
        // If the method add is called with 0 as the first operand and any integer as the second (e.g., 0 and 5), then the method should return the second operand (5). This verifies the additive identity property.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(0, 5);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_as_second_operand() {
        // If the method add is called with any integer as the first operand and 0 as the second (e.g., 5 and 0), then the method should return the first operand (5). This verifies the additive identity property.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(5, 0);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_zero_to_zero() {
        // If the method add is called with 0 for both operands, then the method should return 0. This is a specific edge case of the additive identity.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(0, 0);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_integer_max_value_with_zero() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MAX_VALUE and 0, then the method should return Integer.MAX_VALUE. This verifies behavior at the upper boundary of the int type.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 0);
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_integer_min_value_with_zero() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MIN_VALUE and 0, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE. This verifies behavior at the lower boundary of the int type.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 0);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_integer_max_value_with_one_overflows() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MAX_VALUE and 1, then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE. Since the documentation does not specify overflow handling, this tests the default Java integer overflow wrap-around behavior.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MAX_VALUE, 1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_add_integer_min_value_with_negative_one_overflows() {
        // If the method add is called with Integer.MIN_VALUE and -1, then the method should return Integer.MAX_VALUE. Since the documentation does not specify overflow handling, this tests the default Java integer overflow wrap-around behavior.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.add(Integer.MIN_VALUE, -1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, result);
    }

package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here

public class CalculatorTest {
    @Test
    public void test_subtract_positive_numbers_returns_positive_difference() {
        // If the method is called with two positive integers where the first is larger than the second (e.g., a=10, b=5), then the method should return the positive difference (5). This tests the basic functionality described as 'Returns the difference a - b'.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(10, 5);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_equal_numbers_returns_zero() {
        // If the method is called with two identical integers (e.g., a=5, b=5), then the method should return 0. This verifies the boundary case where the difference is zero.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(5, 5);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_larger_from_smaller_returns_negative_difference() {
        // If the method is called with two positive integers where the second is larger than the first (e.g., a=5, b=10), then the method should return the negative difference (-5). This ensures correct handling of sign changes in the result.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(5, 10);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_zero_from_zero_returns_zero() {
        // If the method is called with zero for both arguments (a=0, b=0), then the method should return 0. This tests the behavior at the neutral element of addition/subtraction.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(0, 0);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_zero_from_positive_returns_positive() {
        // If the method is called with a positive integer and zero (a=7, b=0), then the method should return the original positive integer (7). This verifies that subtracting zero has no effect on the first operand.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(7, 0);
        assertEquals(7, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_positive_from_zero_returns_negative() {
        // If the method is called with zero and a positive integer (a=0, b=7), then the method should return the negation of the second integer (-7). This tests subtraction from the neutral element.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(0, 7);
        assertEquals(-7, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_two_negative_numbers() {
        // If the method is called with two negative integers (e.g., a=-5, b=-3), then the method should return the correct difference (-2). This verifies arithmetic rules for negative operands.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(-5, -3);
        assertEquals(-2, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_negative_from_positive_results_in_addition() {
        // If the method is called with a positive integer and a negative integer (a=5, b=-3), then the method should return the sum of their absolute values (8), effectively testing that 'a - (-b)' equals 'a + b'.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(5, -3);
        assertEquals(8, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_positive_from_negative_increases_magnitude() {
        // If the method is called with a negative integer and a positive integer (a=-5, b=3), then the method should return a more negative result (-8). This tests the combination of signs resulting in a larger magnitude negative number.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(-5, 3);
        assertEquals(-8, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_min_from_max_int_handles_overflow_correctly() {
        // If the method is called with Integer.MIN_VALUE and 1 (a=Integer.MIN_VALUE, b=1), then the method should return Integer.MAX_VALUE due to two's complement integer underflow. This ensures the implementation relies on standard Java int arithmetic without throwing exceptions, as no exception handling is documented.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_max_int_by_negative_one_handles_overflow() {
        // If the method is called with Integer.MAX_VALUE and -1 (a=Integer.MAX_VALUE, b=-1), then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE due to two's complement integer overflow. This verifies that the method does not throw an ArithmeticException or similar for overflow cases, adhering to standard primitive int behavior.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, -1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }


    @Test
    public void test_subtract_max_int_from_min_int_overflow_behavior() {
        // If the method is called with Integer.MIN_VALUE and Integer.MAX_VALUE (a=Integer.MIN_VALUE, b=Integer.MAX_VALUE), then the method should return -1. This tests the specific overflow result of subtracting the largest positive integer from the smallest negative integer.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        assertEquals(-1, result);
    }

package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

// add all other needed imports here
public class CalculatorTest {

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_positive_numbers_returns_positive_difference() {
        // If the method is called with two positive integers where the first is larger than the second (e.g., a=10, b=5), then the method should return the positive difference (5). This tests the basic functionality described as 'Returns the difference a - b'.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(10, 5);
        assertEquals(5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_equal_numbers_returns_zero() {
        // If the method is called with two identical integers (e.g., a=5, b=5), then the method should return 0. This verifies the boundary case where the difference is zero.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(5, 5);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_larger_from_smaller_returns_negative_difference() {
        // If the method is called with two positive integers where the second is larger than the first (e.g., a=5, b=10), then the method should return the negative difference (-5). This ensures correct handling of sign changes in the result.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(5, 10);
        assertEquals(-5, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_zero_from_zero_returns_zero() {
        // If the method is called with zero for both arguments (a=0, b=0), then the method should return 0. This tests the behavior at the neutral element of addition/subtraction.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(0, 0);
        assertEquals(0, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_zero_from_positive_returns_positive() {
        // If the method is called with a positive integer and zero (a=7, b=0), then the method should return the original positive integer (7). This verifies that subtracting zero has no effect on the first operand.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(7, 0);
        assertEquals(7, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_positive_from_zero_returns_negative() {
        // If the method is called with zero and a positive integer (a=0, b=7), then the method should return the negation of the second integer (-7). This tests subtraction from the neutral element.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(0, 7);
        assertEquals(-7, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_two_negative_numbers() {
        // If the method is called with two negative integers (e.g., a=-5, b=-3), then the method should return the correct difference (-2). This verifies arithmetic rules for negative operands.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(-5, -3);
        assertEquals(-2, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_negative_from_positive_results_in_addition() {
        // If the method is called with a positive integer and a negative integer (a=5, b=-3), then the method should return the sum of their absolute values (8), effectively testing that 'a - (-b)' equals 'a + b'.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(5, -3);
        assertEquals(8, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_positive_from_negative_increases_magnitude() {
        // If the method is called with a negative integer and a positive integer (a=-5, b=3), then the method should return a more negative result (-8). This tests the combination of signs resulting in a larger magnitude negative number.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(-5, 3);
        assertEquals(-8, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_min_from_max_int_handles_overflow_correctly() {
        // If the method is called with Integer.MIN_VALUE and 1 (a=Integer.MIN_VALUE, b=1), then the method should return Integer.MAX_VALUE due to two's complement integer underflow. This ensures the implementation relies on standard Java int arithmetic without throwing exceptions, as no exception handling is documented.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MAX_VALUE, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_max_int_by_negative_one_handles_overflow() {
        // If the method is called with Integer.MAX_VALUE and -1 (a=Integer.MAX_VALUE, b=-1), then the method should return Integer.MIN_VALUE due to two's complement integer overflow. This verifies that the method does not throw an ArithmeticException or similar for overflow cases, adhering to standard primitive int behavior.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(Integer.MAX_VALUE, -1);
        assertEquals(Integer.MIN_VALUE, result);
    }

    @Test
    public void test_subtract_max_int_from_min_int_overflow_behavior() {
        // If the method is called with Integer.MIN_VALUE and Integer.MAX_VALUE (a=Integer.MIN_VALUE, b=Integer.MAX_VALUE), then the method should return -1. This tests the specific overflow result of subtracting the largest positive integer from the smallest negative integer.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        int result = calculator.subtract(Integer.MIN_VALUE, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        assertEquals(-1, result);
    }
}

    77	        int result = calculator.subtract(5, -3);

package example;

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;
import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertDoesNotThrow;

import java.io.ByteArrayOutputStream;
import java.io.PrintStream;

// add all other needed imports here

public class CalculatorTest {
    @Test
    public void test_dummy2CompletesWithoutException() {
        // If dummy2() is invoked on an instance of the class, then no checked or unchecked exceptions (such as RuntimeException or Error) should be thrown during execution.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        assertDoesNotThrow(() -> calculator.dummy2());
    }


    @Test
    public void test_dummy2DoesNotModifyInstanceState() {
        // If dummy2() is invoked on an object with initialized mutable fields, then the values of all instance fields must remain identical to their state before the method call, verifying the 'does nothing' requirement regarding object state.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        // Since Calculator has no visible fields, we can only verify that calling dummy2() doesn't throw
        // and the object remains usable. For a truly stateless dummy method, this test verifies
        // that the method completes without side effects on the instance.
        assertDoesNotThrow(() -> calculator.dummy2());
    }


    @Test
    public void test_dummy2DoesNotModifyStaticState() {
        // If dummy2() is invoked, then any static fields within the class or related classes must remain unchanged, ensuring no global side effects occur as implied by 'has no code'.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        assertDoesNotThrow(() -> calculator.dummy2());
    }


    @Test
    public void test_dummy2ProducesNoOutput() {
        // If dummy2() is invoked while monitoring standard output and error streams, then no data should be written to System.out or System.err, verifying the absence of print statements or logging.
        Calculator calculator = new Calculator();
        
        ByteArrayOutputStream outContent = new ByteArrayOutputStream();
        ByteArrayOutputStream errContent = new ByteArrayOutputStream();
        
        PrintStream originalOut = System.out;
        PrintStream originalErr = System.err;
        
        try {
            System.setOut(new PrintStream(outContent));
            System.setErr(new PrintStream(errContent));
            
            calculator.dummy2();
            
            assertEquals("", outContent.toString(), "No output should be written to System.out");
            assertEquals("", errContent.toString(), "No output should be written to System.err");
        } finally {
            System.setOut(originalOut);
            System.setErr(originalErr);
        }
    }


}
